\begin{document}

\title{\vspace{-1.2cm}\bfseries Slot-Chain: A Preconfirmation Protocol\\[0.15em]
Built on Per-Slot Signature Chains\\[0.5em]
\large\mdseries v2 Design Specification}
\author{}
\date{Draft v1.50 \quad\textperiodcentered\quad August 2026}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{fancy}

\begin{abstract}
\noindent
This document specifies the successor design (v2) of the Taiko preconfirmation
protocol. L2 time is divided into one-second slots; a lookahead schedule,
published two epochs in advance, assigns each slot to a unique builder. The
builder produces a block and signs it, and because each signature covers the
parent hash, the not-yet-landed blocks form a \emph{signature chain}. Submitting
such a chain to L1 together with a validity proof is called \emph{landing}: an
aggregator elected by a perpetual auction performs it in the normal case, but
anyone may perform it when the aggregator is absent, because a block's authority
comes from its builder's signature rather than from whoever lands it. Finality
is given by the proof system together with a deterministic \emph{settlement
window}: among the candidates that have landed and been proven, the heaviest one
is finalized when the window closes at a predetermined L1 height. We give the
trust model, the liveness accounting, the forced-inclusion floor, the slashing
rules and the parameter geometry, and we state the accepted residual risks and
the remaining open items explicitly rather than eliding them.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents
\clearpage
\setcounter{section}{-1}
\begin{designnote}
\textbf{Status of this document.} This is the successor design to the Taiko preconfirmation protocol, superseding the earlier v15 design line (perpetual auction plus epoch-level determination; its full text and review record are preserved in the git history, and the conclusions worth retaining are given in \texttt{\texttt{legacy-\allowbreak{}summary.md}}). The design originates in the owner's instruction of 2026-08-25: adopt a sub-epoch failover granularity in which rotating builders produce and sign one block per slot, the signatures are strung into a chain, and the result is verified by the proof system. This document is the English edition; the Chinese edition is the normative source (see the note at the head of this file).
\end{designnote}

\begin{designnote}
\textbf{How to read this document.} The main text (\hyperref[sec:0]{\S0}–\hyperref[sec:12]{\S12} together with Appendices A–C) is the specification proper and can simply be read in order; a reader in a hurry to grasp the trunk can build a full picture from the \hyperref[sec:0]{\S0} one-page summary and the \hyperref[sec:9]{\S9} liveness accounting table. The many parenthetical notes in the main text of the form "\revnote{1}" or "\revnote{2}" are audit traces of the design process --- they record which round of adversarial review prompted a given rule --- and a first reading may skip them outright without any loss of understanding; return to them when the provenance of a particular rule needs to be traced. The complete per-version change history (from v1.31 onward, including the findings and fixes of every review round) is collected in \hyperref[app:D]{Appendix~D} and is no longer placed ahead of the main text.
\end{designnote}

\section[One-page summary]{One-page summary}\label{sec:0}
\textbf{In one sentence:} L2 has one slot per second, and the lookahead --- which states "who holds the right to produce a block in this second" --- is published two epochs in advance. The builder whose turn it is produces a block and signs it, and the signature covers the parent block hash; the blocks, which are not yet on chain, are thereby strung into a signature chain. The act of packing this chain onto L1 together with a validity proof is called landing: in normal operation the aggregator produced by the auction is responsible for it, but when the aggregator goes offline anyone may do it --- because a block's authority comes from the builder's signature, not from the lander.

The complete path from block production to finality:

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
  \node[scnode, text width=58mm] (B)
        {Builder (rotates by lookahead)\\produces and signs a block each slot};
  \node[scnode, text width=58mm, below=12mm of B] (SC)
        {Signature chain (P2P, not on chain)\\a signed block is itself the preconfirmation};
  \node[scnode, scfill, text width=58mm, below=14mm of SC] (CAND) {L1 candidate batch};
  \node[scnode, text width=58mm, below=9mm of CAND] (WIN)
        {Settlement window $W_{\mathrm{settle}}$\\heaviest proven candidate\\in the window wins};
  \node[scnode, scfill, text width=58mm, below=10mm of WIN] (WF)
        {window-final\\withdrawals and bridging\\execute from this state};
  \node[scnode, scfillb, text width=58mm, below=10mm of WF] (LF)
        {L1-final};

  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (AGG) at ($(CAND.west)+(-4.0,1.0)$) {Aggregator\\(untrusted)};
  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (ANY) at ($(CAND.west)+(-4.0,-1.1)$) {Anyone\\(fallback)};

  \draw[scarr] (B) -- node[sclabl, right=1mm, text width=40mm]
        {signature covers \texttt{parent\_hash}} (SC);
  \draw[scarr] (SC) -- node[sclabl, right=1mm, text width=40mm]
        {batched $+$ validity proof,\\one L1 transaction} (CAND);
  \draw[scarr] (CAND) -- (WIN);
  \draw[scarr] (WIN) -- node[sclabl, right=1mm, text width=40mm] {deterministic close} (WF);
  \draw[scarr] (WF) -- node[sclabl, right=1mm, text width=40mm]
        {landing tx reaches $F_{\mathrm{l1}}$ depth} (LF);

  \draw[scdarr] (AGG.east) -- node[sclab, pos=0.45, above=0pt, text width=22mm]
        {normal landing} (CAND.west);
  \draw[scdarr] (ANY.east) -- node[sclab, pos=0.45, below=0pt, text width=22mm]
        {fallback past threshold} (CAND.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The complete path from block production to finality: builder signatures produce a signature chain that never touches the chain, anyone may land it on L1 together with a validity proof to become a candidate, and once the settlement window closes it becomes window-final and then L1-final. Landing carries no privilege, so the aggregator and the fallback lander are interchangeable.}
\label{fig:1}
\end{figure}

\textbf{This structure solves three things at once:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Second-scale liveness.} A builder going offline costs only its own slot (about 1 second), and the next builder simply continues producing blocks on top of the last visible chain head. There is no window in which "one participant goes offline and the chain stops for tens of minutes".
\item \textbf{No L2 consensus is required.} "Who should produce the block in this slot" is decided by the lookahead, and "whether this block is legal" is decided by pure-function rules inside the proving circuit (verify the signature, verify the lookahead assignment, verify the link) --- anyone reading L1 computes the same answer, with no voting and no arbiter.
\item \textbf{Landing carries no privilege, and the aggregator is not a single point of failure.} The signature chain carries its own authority; landing is merely transport. If the designated aggregator times out without landing, anyone may pick the signature chain up, land it, and collect a reward out of the aggregator's bond.
\end{enumerate}
\textbf{What is kept and what is removed (relative to v15):}

\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{p{0.30\linewidth}X}
\toprule
\textbf{Kept} & \textbf{Removed} \\
\midrule
The perpetual auction skeleton for the aggregator seat & Epoch boundary commitments (EBC) and the once-per-epoch irreversible determination \\
The forced-inclusion queue, with greatly simplified rules (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}) & The default derivation rule (\hyperref[sec:6.8]{\S6.8} default derivation) \\
The "party at fault pays, permissionless substitution" model (fault-paid permissionless fallback) & Total Anarchy mode (\hyperref[sec:9]{\S9}) --- landing itself can already be backstopped permissionlessly, so it is no longer needed \\
The tiered bond / slashing concept & \texttt{K\_\allowbreak{}empty}, availability certificates (AC), the \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}cancel} cancellation cascade, and the rest of the machinery supporting the epoch determination \\
The per-block anchor approach to L1→L2 bridging & Handover and seat-switching arbitration mechanisms (replaced by circuit rules) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

\textbf{Timeline illustration (slot = 1 second, epoch = 384 slots)} --- going offline leaves only a gap, and the chain does not stop:

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (b1) at (0,0)     {slot $s$\\builder A\\produces};
  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (b2) at (3.0,0)   {slot $s{+}1$\\builder B\\produces};
  \node[scdnote, scfill, text width=22mm, minimum height=6mm] (gp) at (6.0,0)
        {slot $s{+}2$\\builder C offline\\(gap)};
  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (b3) at (9.0,0)   {slot $s{+}3$\\builder D\\produces};
  \node[scsm, text width=22mm] (b4) at (12.0,0)  {$\cdots$\\chain continues\\second by second};

  \draw[scarr] (b1) -- (b2);
  \draw[scarr] (b3) -- (b4);
  \draw[scline, dash pattern=on 1.2pt off 1.2pt, gray] (b2.east) -- (gp.west);
  \draw[scline, dash pattern=on 1.2pt off 1.2pt, gray] (gp.east) -- (b3.west);

  \draw[scdarr] (b2.north) to[bend left=26]
        node[sclab, above=0.5mm, text width=76mm]
        {Gap: no proof required, nobody is slashed; the \texttt{parent\_hash}\\of $s{+}3$ points straight at the block of $s{+}1$}
        (b3.north);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A single builder going offline leaves nothing but a one-slot gap in the timeline: the next builder takes the last visible block as its parent and carries on, so the chain never stalls.}
\label{fig:2}
\end{figure}

The relationship between landing and fallback (details in \hyperref[sec:6]{\S6}): anyone may take a contiguous segment of the signature chain plus a validity proof and land it on L1 in a single transaction, making it a candidate; the heaviest proven candidate within the settlement window \texttt{W\_\allowbreak{}settle} is final at the window close (\hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6}). When the aggregator times out without landing, anyone may land, and the reward is paid out of the aggregator's bond (\hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}).

\section[Design goals and non-goals]{Design goals and non-goals}\label{sec:1}
\textbf{Goals:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{[G1] Second-scale continuity of ordering.} If any single participant (a builder or the aggregator) goes offline, the interruption of ordering service perceptible to users lasts at most a few slots (seconds), not a few epochs (minutes to hours).
\item \textbf{[G2] No L2 consensus.} The lookahead, legality and canonicality are all pure functions of L1 data and circuit rules; L2 nodes neither vote nor arbitrate among themselves. This inherits the spirit of v15's I1: derivation is a deterministic function of L1 data.
\item \textbf{[G3] Landing carries no privilege.} Moving an already-signed chain onto L1 requires no identity whatsoever; the designated aggregator is merely an efficiency arrangement (avoiding gas competition and duplicated proving), not an arrangement of power.
\item \textbf{[G4] Proof plus a deterministic window is finality \revnote{3}.} A landed batch carries a validity proof, which L1 verifies on the spot, and the batch becomes a candidate; at the close of the settlement window the heaviest proven candidate is finalized --- there is still no optimistic period, no fraud proof and no arbitration, and there is no intermediate "proposed but unproven" state, so the entire body of machinery built around such an intermediate state (sealing deadlines, cancellation cascades, and most of the timing exemptions) is unnecessary.
\item \textbf{[G5] The inclusion floor is retained.} The forced-inclusion queue is retained: even if all builders collude to censor, users and bridge messages still have an L1 forced path (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}). This "unconditional" guarantee currently carries one blocking-level gap \revnote{4}: forced entries use ordinary L2 nonces, so a censoring party can accept a conflicting transaction bearing the same nonce and thereby have the entry consumed and discarded (the nonce preemption of \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}, item 12 of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}). Until this open item is settled by one of the three candidate remedies in \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}, the "unconditional" character of the forced path holds only for entries that cannot be preempted by nonce --- bridging and exit messages, which are constructed by the bridge contract and do not go through a user nonce, are of this kind, whereas an ordinary user's forced transaction is for now not. Wherever \hyperref[sec:0]{\S0}, \hyperref[sec:1]{\S1} and \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7} speak of an "unconditional floor", the phrase is to be read subject to this note.
\item \textbf{[G6] A low barrier to entry.} The builder bond is priced at the order of magnitude of "the extractable value of a single slot", far below the full collateral for one seat in v15.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Trust model (normative)\revnote{5}:}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Builders: honest majority.} We assume that within each lookahead window (on the order of an epoch), most of the scheduled builders honestly follow the protocol (producing blocks on time, broadcasting block headers and block bodies, and extending the highest available chain head).
\item \textbf{Aggregators: untrusted.} An aggregator may misbehave arbitrarily --- delaying, truncating, choosing among forks, colluding with a minority of builders --- and the protocol must, under such conditions, still hold the bounds given in the \hyperref[sec:9]{\S9} table. The scope of those bounds \revnote{6}: the bounds in \hyperref[sec:9]{\S9} are liveness bounds (advancing finality, seat turnover); the exposure of preconfirmation safety to collusion between the aggregator and malicious builders --- isolation without slashing, a single-block deep skip of depth ≤ \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max − 1}, and a minority coalition relaying a sparse branch to a depth that can reach the length of the unlanded tail (≈ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} + fallback response) --- is the safety residual risk explicitly priced in \hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4}, and it falls outside the "bounded" promise.
\item \textbf{L1: safe and live.} The standard assumption of every L2. Added to it is one assumption that this design explicitly acknowledges, the Bounded-Inclusion Assumption \revnote{7}: a candidate or landing transaction that pays a sufficient fee is included within \texttt{T\_\allowbreak{}include,\allowbreak{}max} L1 blocks, and the parameters satisfy \texttt{T\_\allowbreak{}include,\allowbreak{}max < min(\allowbreak{}W\_\allowbreak{}settle − P\_\allowbreak{}prove,\allowbreak{}max − margin,\allowbreak{} D\_\allowbreak{}anchor\_\allowbreak{}max − D\_\allowbreak{}anchor − Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag − P\_\allowbreak{}prove,\allowbreak{}max)} (the setter relations of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}). Targeted L1 censorship exceeding that bound is out-of-model --- in that case a better candidate may miss the close and a recovery block's anchor may expire --- and every statement that relies on "robustness under temporary censorship" (including the recovery bound of \hyperref[sec:6.4]{\S6.4}) is uniformly conditional on this assumption. This gathers the same timing dependency, implicit at various layers from v1.28 to v1.40, into a single stated assumption; it is not newly introduced trust.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{The honest majority is an economic and social assumption, not one enforced by the protocol \revnote{8}}: the draw is weighted by bond and \texttt{w\_\allowbreak{}max} is no defense against Sybils (\hyperref[sec:3.2]{\S3.2}), so a single capital holder could in principle buy up the majority of the weight and push the system out of the model --- of the same kind as the honest-majority assumption of any PoS chain. This design does not claim that the builder role is entirely trustless or entirely permissionless; this is the trust model explicitly accepted by the owner (2026-08-25), and what is bought in exchange is not paying the cost of protocol-level DA to cover collusion by all participants (the cost identity is given in Appendix A-3). Each promise is calibrated accordingly: the in-model bounds are given in the \hyperref[sec:9]{\S9} table, while out-of-model only the forced path is guaranteed. A note on the acceptance criterion \revnote{9}: if the acceptance criterion is "no role may be trusted, everything fully permissionless", this design neither satisfies it nor attempts to --- the acceptance criterion is the trust model accepted by the owner in this section; the user-facing promises, graded into three tiers, are stated in \hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4}.

How much of the assumption each mechanism actually consumes (written out, to keep the assumption from being quietly strengthened):

\begin{itemize}
\item Finality liveness (fallback landing always having a chain available to land) requires only ≥ 1 honest builder per window (\hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}) --- weaker than the assumption, so there is margin;
\item The frequency with which preconfirmations are isolated (skips, body withholding) is bounded by the share of dishonest builders --- this is where "majority" is used, and it is load-bearing there: skipping is profitable for the skipper \revnote{10}, not a behavior that extinguishes itself for being unprofitable;
\item Collusion of the entire builder set together with the aggregator (block bodies kept private, the chain landable by no one) lies outside the model: the protocol provides no finality guarantee in that case \revnote{11}. The forced path (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}) is nevertheless retained as out-of-model defense in depth --- even if the assumption is broken, forced transactions and bridge messages still operate independently on L1 data alone, so the user's right to exit does not lapse with the model;
\item \textbf{Preconfirmation safety for the tail additionally depends on the completeness of the lander's view \revnote{12}}: a lander that is honest but eclipsed or blinded will land the wrong tail and revoke the preconfirmations of a heavier tail that it never saw. The mitigation is "landing depth ≥ propagation settling" (\hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2}), which compresses this risk down to "equivocation (slashable) or eclipse (the P2P layer)"; together with "the honest majority is an economic assumption" and "liveness under a Byzantine aggregator is conditional on fallback being economically viable", it belongs to the explicitly stated non-pure-protocol dependencies.
\end{itemize}
\textbf{Non-goals (not addressed in this version):}

\begin{itemize}
\item Per-transaction fair exchange: the preconfirmation receipt is block-level (\hyperref[sec:5]{\S5}).
\item User restitution: slashing is a deterrent, not insurance.
\item Resistance to deep L1 reorgs: as with any L2, a deep L1 reorg can roll back L2 state.
\item A built-in prover market: the aggregator either brings its own proving capability or procures it.
\item \textbf{Collusion of the entire builder set plus the aggregator}: under the trust model above this is an out-of-model situation; only the forced path is retained as defense in depth, and no finality bound is offered.
\end{itemize}
\section[Roles]{Roles}\label{sec:2}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Builder}: the set of addresses that have registered with the L1 contract and posted a bond. When the lookahead brings a builder's own slot around, it produces a block, signs it, and broadcasts it on the P2P network. The builder is the preconfer: the block it signs is itself the preconfirmation commitment for the transactions inside that block.
\item \textbf{Aggregator}: the service provider that holds the sole seat by way of the perpetual auction. It has exactly one duty: to pack the signature chain into a batch, generate a validity proof, and land it on L1. It does not build, does not order, and holds no discretionary power over content --- the only residual discretion is "which prefix to land and when to land it", and both are constrained by deadlines and by permissionless fallback (\hyperref[sec:6]{\S6}).
\item \textbf{Fallback lander}: anyone. A fallback lander appears once the aggregator has timed out and is paid under the fault-paid model. This is not a registered role.
\item \textbf{User}: submits transactions to builders in exchange for preconfirmations; when censored, goes by way of the forced-inclusion queue.
\item \textbf{L1 contracts}: the builder registry, the aggregator auction, the forced-inclusion queue, the batch entry point (which verifies proofs), and the slashing entry point.
\item \textbf{The proof system}: the circuit that verifies "this segment of the chain is legal and its state transition is correct". It is the arbiter of this design.
\end{itemize}
\section[Time and scheduling]{Time and scheduling}\label{sec:3}
\subsection[Slots and epochs]{Slots and epochs}\label{sec:3.1}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{slot = 1 second}. The time unit of L2. There is at most one block per slot, and a block's timestamp is identically the starting second of its slot --- the timestamp is not chosen by anyone, it is fixed by the lookahead (which eliminates the entire class of problems around the origin of timestamps found in \hyperref[app:C]{Appendix~C} of v15).
\item \textbf{epoch = 384 slots} (the current value is retained, aligned with L1's 32 × 12 seconds). In this design an epoch is merely a unit of measure (the granularity of lookahead windows and of fee settlement); it no longer carries the mechanism-level meaning of "one determination per epoch".
\end{itemize}
\subsection[The lookahead]{The lookahead}\label{sec:3.2}
\begin{itemize}
\item The lookahead is a mapping \texttt{lookahead(\allowbreak{}slot) → builder address} that gives, for every future slot, the unique holder of the right to produce a block.
\item \textbf{Computation (a pure function)\revnote{13}:} \texttt{lookahead(\allowbreak{}slot) = draw(\allowbreak{}hash(\allowbreak{}seed(\allowbreak{}window),\allowbreak{} slot),\allowbreak{} registry\_\allowbreak{}snapshot(\allowbreak{}window))} --- the seed is taken once per window, not once per slot:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Window partitioning \revnote{14}}: a window is a fixed aligned partition: \texttt{W(\allowbreak{}slot) = floor(\allowbreak{}slot /\allowbreak{} W\_\allowbreak{}size)}, \texttt{W\_\allowbreak{}size = 768} (two L2 epochs); the starting slot of window \texttt{W} is \texttt{W × W\_\allowbreak{}size}, and "the L1 slot corresponding to that start" is converted by the genesis mapping \texttt{L1\_\allowbreak{}slot(\allowbreak{}s) = GENESIS\_\allowbreak{}L1 +\allowbreak{} floor(\allowbreak{}s /\allowbreak{} 12)} (an L2 slot is 1 second, an L1 slot is 12 seconds). The lookahead assignment of any given slot has the same value for every observer at every moment.
\item \textbf{A unique snapshot height}: the snapshot height of window \texttt{W} is \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W) = the L1 slot corresponding to the start of W − D\_\allowbreak{}snap}, where \texttt{D\_\allowbreak{}snap} = the lookahead horizon \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}look} (2 L1 epochs) + the finality distance \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}final} (2 L1 epochs) + margin (1 L1 epoch) = 5 L1 epochs \revnote{15}. The setter invariant: \texttt{D\_\allowbreak{}snap ≥ H\_\allowbreak{}look +\allowbreak{} F\_\allowbreak{}final +\allowbreak{} margin}. It follows that at every moment at which the lookahead is required to be already determined, \texttt{randao\_\allowbreak{}source(\allowbreak{}W) = H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W) ≤ finalized L1 head < start of W} --- there is only one seed, it holds simultaneously for all slots in the window, and the lookahead is fixed before it is used and is unaffected by L1 reorgs.
\item \textbf{The seed}: the RANDAO of the L1 epoch containing \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W)} (provable in-circuit via the EIP-4788 beacon root); the variation between slots is derived by \texttt{hash(\allowbreak{}seed,\allowbreak{} slot)}, so no independent per-slot entropy source is needed.
\item \textbf{The registry snapshot}: the registry state at that same \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W)}; the entry and exit delay (\hyperref[sec:4.1]{\S4.1}) guarantees that the snapshot covers the whole window. The circuit's lookahead check for every block in the window uses \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W)} uniformly, independently of each block's own anchor block \revnote{16}.
\item \textbf{The draw}: deterministic bond-weighted sampling (independent for each slot). The weight of a single address is capped at \texttt{w\_\allowbreak{}max} (initially 20\%). An honest characterization \revnote{17}: \texttt{w\_\allowbreak{}max} only prevents concentration in a single address and is no defense against a Sybil that splits its bond --- large capital need only split across several addresses to recapture the weight, and it costs not one cent more. The real upper bound on concentration is economic (buying weight requires genuinely locking up bond), the same trade-off as v15's acceptance that "the highest bidder can keep on winning". The role of \texttt{w\_\allowbreak{}max} is operational hygiene; it is not a promise of decentralization.
\end{itemize}
\item The full codification of the computation above (the constraint at each step is in the comments; for a runnable version with property tests see \texttt{\texttt{lookahead-\allowbreak{}model.py}}, and the sampling operator is the candidate for settling item 18(b) of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}):
\end{itemize}
\begin{codeblock}
\mbox{}~W\_SIZE~=~768~\#~lookahead~window~=~768~L2~slots~(fixed~aligned~partition,~not~a~sliding~horizon)\\
\mbox{}~H\_LOOK~=~768~\#~lookahead~horizon\\
\mbox{}~D\_SNAP\_L1~=~5~*~32~\#~snapshot~delay~(L1~slots);~invariant~D\_snap~≥~H\_look~+~F\_final~+~margin\\
\mbox{}~W\_MAX~=~0.20~\#~cap~on~a~single~address's~effective~weight~(operational~hygiene;~no~defense~against~a~bond-splitting~Sybil)\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~window\_of(slot):\\
\mbox{}~\#~fixed~aligned~partition:~the~same~slot~maps~to~the~same~window~at~every~moment~and~for~every~observer~(r10-5)\\
\mbox{}~return~slot~//~W\_SIZE\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~snapshot\_height(w):\\
\mbox{}~\#~unique~snapshot~height~=~the~L1~slot~corresponding~to~the~window~start~−~D\_snap.\\
\mbox{}~\#~the~three~terms~of~D\_snap~guarantee:~before~the~lookahead~is~used,~this~height~is~already\\
\mbox{}~\#~L1-finalized~---~neither~the~seed~nor~the~registry~is~affected~by~an~L1~reorg~(r8-1)\\
\mbox{}~return~l1\_slot\_of(w~*~W\_SIZE)~-~D\_SNAP\_L1\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~seed(w):\\
\mbox{}~\#~seed~=~the~RANDAO~of~the~L1~epoch~containing~the~snapshot~height~(provable~in-circuit~via~the~EIP-4788~beacon~root).\\
\mbox{}~\#~taken~once~per~window,~not~once~per~slot:~all~slots~in~the~window~share~one~entropy~source~(r7-1)\\
\mbox{}~return~hash("seed",~randao\_at(snapshot\_height(w)))\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~effective\_weights(registry):\\
\mbox{}~\#~effective~weight~=~min(bond,~w\_max~×~total~bond).~capped~once,~no~iterative~renormalization~---\\
\mbox{}~\#~the~excess~is~voided~rather~than~redistributed;~a~single~linear~pass,~circuit-friendly\\
\mbox{}~total~=~sum(registry.values())\\
\mbox{}~return~\{a:~min(b,~W\_MAX~*~total)~for~a,~b~in~registry.items()\}\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~lookahead(slot):\\
\mbox{}~\#~lookahead(slot)~=~draw(hash(seed(window),~slot),~registry\_snapshot(window)).\\
\mbox{}~\#~the~registry~snapshot~and~the~seed~are~taken~at~the~same~height~(r1-9~uniqueness);~the~variation~between~slots~is~derived~from~the~hash\\
\mbox{}~w~=~window\_of(slot)\\
\mbox{}~reg~=~registry\_at(snapshot\_height(w))\\
\mbox{}~r~=~hash(seed(w),~slot)\\
\mbox{}~return~weighted\_pick(reg,~r)\\
\mbox{}\\
\mbox{}~def~weighted\_pick(registry,~r):\\
\mbox{}~\#~deterministic~weighted~sampling~(the~candidate~operator~for~settling~§12-18(b)):\\
\mbox{}~\#~1.~addresses~in~a~fixed~order~by~registration~index~(an~order~intrinsic~to~the~snapshot,~preventing~grinding~on~the~registered~name);\\
\mbox{}~\#~2.~prefix~sums~of~the~capped~weights~(fixed-point~integer~wei,~no~floating~point);\\
\mbox{}~\#~3.~x~=~r~mod~total~weight;~whichever~prefix~interval~x~falls~into~determines~the~address~selected.\\
\mbox{}~\#~probability~of~being~selected~=~share~of~effective~weight;~in-circuit~=~one~prefix-sum~pass~+~one~modulo~+~one~lookup\\
\mbox{}~eff~=~effective\_weights(registry)\\
\mbox{}~addrs~=~sorted\_by\_registration\_index(registry)\\
\mbox{}~x~=~r~\%~sum(eff[a]~for~a~in~addrs)\\
\mbox{}~acc~=~0\\
\mbox{}~for~a~in~addrs:\\
\mbox{}~acc~+=~eff[a]\\
\mbox{}~if~x~<~acc:\\
\mbox{}~return~a\\
\end{codeblock}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Lookahead availability}: at any moment, the lookahead for at least the next \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}look = 768} slots (about 12.8 minutes) is already determined and computable by anyone (the remainder of the current window plus the whole of the next window; the horizon term of \texttt{D\_\allowbreak{}snap} guarantees that the next window's snapshot is already finalized). The temporal geometry of snapshots, windows and the lookahead horizon:
\end{itemize}
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
  % time axis
  \draw[scarr] (0,0) -- (13.6,0);
  \node[font=\scriptsize, anchor=west] at (13.7,0) {};
  \foreach \x in {2.2,7.4,12.6}{\draw[scline] (\x,-0.16) -- (\x,0.16);}

  % below the axis: three instants
  \node[scnote, text width=52mm, anchor=north] at (2.2,-0.35)
        {Snapshot point $H_{\mathrm{snap}}(W)$\\$=$ start of window $W$ $-\,D_{\mathrm{snap}}$\\
         takes the RANDAO seed\\$+$ registry snapshot\\(already L1-final, immune to reorgs)};
  \node[scsm, text width=34mm, anchor=north] at (7.4,-0.35)
        {Start of window $W$\\slot $= W \times W_{\mathrm{size}}$};
  \node[scsm, text width=26mm, anchor=north] at (12.6,-0.35)
        {End of window $W$};

  % above the axis: two intervals
  \draw[scdim] (2.2,0.55) -- (7.4,0.55);
  \node[sclab, text width=48mm, anchor=south] at (4.8,0.65)
        {$D_{\mathrm{snap}} \ge H_{\mathrm{look}} + F_{\mathrm{final}} +$ margin\\($=5$ L1 epoch)};
  \draw[scdim] (7.4,0.55) -- (12.6,0.55);
  \node[sclab, text width=48mm, anchor=south] at (10.0,0.65)
        {$W_{\mathrm{size}} = 768$ slot\\fixed aligned partition,\\not a sliding window};

  % lookahead horizon (dashed)
  \draw[scdarr] (7.4,-2.75) -- (12.6,-2.75);
  \node[sclab, text width=56mm, anchor=north] at (10.0,-2.85)
        {Lookahead is fixed before it is used:\\at every instant at least\\$H_{\mathrm{look}} = 768$ slot are computable};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The time geometry of the snapshot point, the lookahead window and the lookahead horizon: entropy and the registry are frozen once and for all at $D_{\mathrm{snap}}$ before the window starts, so at every instant at least one full window of lookahead can be computed by anyone.}
\label{fig:3}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Why "the hash of the most recent proof" is not mixed in as entropy \revnote{18}:} one and the same statement can generate an unbounded number of byte-distinct valid proofs (proof generation carries randomness of its own), so a proof hash is entropy that the prover producing it can re-grind at zero cost --- mixing it in would amount to handing part of the control over the lookahead to the aggregator. Pure L1 beacon randomness can be biased by L1 proposers by roughly one bit each, but it is good enough for a use such as the lookahead and introduces no new controlling party. The principle: entropy comes only from the L1 consensus layer, and the moment at which its value is taken is earlier than the lookahead window.
\item \textbf{A known cost, stated openly:} a public lookahead means that "who produces the block in the next second" is public, so a targeted DoS against a builder about to produce a block is theoretically feasible. Mitigations: a slot is only 1 second long (the attack window is minuscule), builders can use sentry or private networks, and a slot that is DoS'd merely turns into a gap (\hyperref[sec:5.3]{\S5.3}). Ethereum itself runs with the same public lookahead.
\end{itemize}
\section[Builders]{Builders}\label{sec:4}
\subsection[Registry]{Registry}\label{sec:4.1}
\begin{itemize}
\item Maintained by an L1 contract. Registration requires posting a bond \texttt{B\_\allowbreak{}builder}, which serves two purposes (one account, two red lines):
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Equivocation slashing allowance \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq}} (ETH). The pricing base is the cumulative exposure over a window, not a single slot \revnote{19}: before a builder has been slashed for the first time and that slashing has taken effect (\texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} in \hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3}), it can pre-sign both forks in bulk for every slot it holds in the lookahead for the current window, so \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq ≥ the maximum number of lookahead slots held by that builder within one exposure horizon × the worst-\allowbreak{}case extractable value per slot × a safety factor}, where the exposure horizon = the remaining lookahead window + \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} + a margin for detection and submission latency \revnote{20}. Computed with \texttt{w\_\allowbreak{}max} = 20\% and a window of 768 slot, the magnitude is a cumulative exposure on the order of one to two hundred slots. The legitimate way to push this number down is to shorten the slashing-effectiveness delay and the detection latency, not to pretend that the exposure is only one slot. The concrete valuation method is calibration content under item 2 of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}.
\item \textbf{Nuisance buffer}: a small amount covering the handling cost of sporadic violations.
\item \textbf{The honest upper bound of \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} \revnote{21}}: \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} is priced against an estimate of the extractable value visible to the protocol; for value external to the protocol (bribes, cross-domain positions, downstream value on the bridge) the protocol can give no upper bound --- so the equivocation deterrent is a "deterrent priced against an estimate", not a guarantee against arbitrary external incentives. Any party that relies on commitments exceeding the size of the bond must price that residual risk itself (the same honesty clause as "deterrence, not compensation" in v15 \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2}; compensating users is a non-goal to begin with, \hyperref[sec:1]{\S1}). Evidence is idempotent: multiple pieces of equivocation evidence against the same builder trigger slashing only once (\texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} is confiscated in full, in one shot, and any later evidence is a no-op) --- the total deterrence ceiling for a single builder is exactly \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq}, which is precisely why \hyperref[sec:4.1]{\S4.1} prices it against the exposure horizon as a whole and why \hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3} uses \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} to freeze the builder's remaining slots into gaps as quickly as possible.
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Entry/exit delay and bond retention period \revnote{22}}: after an exit request, every slot already scheduled to the builder in the lookahead remains valid (it either produces a block or becomes a gap) --- any lookahead entry that has already entered a window's snapshot \texttt{H\_\allowbreak{}snap(\allowbreak{}W)} is valid throughout that window without exception, and does not lapse even if that address subsequently (after the snapshot height) exits the registry (the earlier blanket statement that "the lookahead never contains an address that is no longer registered" was ambiguous: an address that exits after the snapshot does still appear in the already-fixed lookahead, and the correct formulation is "its scheduled slots remain valid and are handled as either a block or a gap"); moreover, the actual unlocking of the bond must wait until all of that builder's slashing exposure has expired --- setter invariant: \textbf{exit delay ≥ the distance to its last scheduled slot + \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} + a margin for detection and submission latency}, the same formula as the exposure horizon of \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} above. Otherwise a builder could equivocate in bulk shortly before exiting and reclaim its bond ahead of the evidence being submitted and taking effect, zeroing out the deterrent.
\item Registry capacity cap \texttt{N\_\allowbreak{}max} (initially 64): decentralized enough, while keeping lookahead verification inside the circuit cheap. Applicants beyond the cap compete for seats ranked by bond (detailed rules pending, \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}).
\end{itemize}
\subsection[Block production and signing]{Block production and signing}\label{sec:4.2}
\begin{itemize}
\item The builder whose turn it is at slot \texttt{s} constructs a block header:
\end{itemize}
\begin{codeblock}
\mbox{}~header~=~(chainId,~slot,~parent\_hash,~anchor,~final\_ref(tier~(ii)~blocks~only,~zero~otherwise),\\
\mbox{}~txs\_root,~state\_root\_claim(optional),~forced\_root,~coinbase,~gas\_used,~...)\\
\mbox{}~sig~=~the~builder's~signature~over~keccak(header)\\
\end{codeblock}

Key fields:

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{slot}: the slot number of this block; \texttt{timestamp} is uniquely determined by it and does not exist separately.
\item \texttt{anchor}: the L1 block referenced by this block (the freshness baseline and the L1→L2 consumption point, \hyperref[sec:8]{\S8})\revnote{23}.
\item \texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash}: the hash of the parent block header. Having the signature cover parent\_hash is a central element of this design: the choice of parent (including "whom to skip") is an explicit act that the builder has signed, not a fuzzy state that can be repudiated afterwards \revnote{24}. The link uses the hash of the parent block header rather than the parent's signature --- the hash is structurally necessary (it defines the chain), whereas putting the parent's signature into the child block as well is redundant (the circuit verifies every block's signature anyway); it may serve as an evidence-packaging convention at the P2P layer, but it is not a consensus rule.
\item \texttt{forced\_\allowbreak{}root}: the commitment to the forced entries contained in this block (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}).
\end{itemize}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Signing-domain uniqueness invariant \revnote{25}}: the canonical digest of the block header and the signature scheme must be one shared object --- the direct verification by the L1 slashing contract (\hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3}) and the validity verification in the circuit (\hyperref[sec:5.1]{\S5.1}) verify exactly the same bytes, the same hash, and the same curve. If BLS (via the EIP-2537 precompile) or a SNARK-friendly hash is adopted to reduce circuit cost, both sides must be switched at the same time. The choice of key system is therefore a blocking open item (item 6 of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}) that must be settled before the remaining parameters.
\item The builder broadcasts (block header + signature + block body) over P2P. This signed block is itself the preconfirmation: the receipt given to the user = (block header, builder's signature, inclusion proof of the transaction) --- the receipt contains the block header hash and \texttt{txs\_\allowbreak{}root}, and is publicly forwardable evidence (used in \hyperref[sec:5.5]{\S5.5}).
\item The decision procedure of the two-tier parent rule (the main statement and its exceptions are given in the next item):
\end{itemize}
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
  \node[scsm, text width=66mm] (S)
        {The builder of slot $s$ picks parent block $P$\\(requires $P.\mathrm{slot} < s$)};
  \node[scdec, text width=28mm, below=12mm of S] (G)
        {gap $s - P.\mathrm{slot}$\\$\le G_{\max}$ ?\\($G_{\max} = 64$ slot)};
  \node[scsm, scfill, text width=58mm, right=10mm of G] (T1)
        {Tier (i), normal block\\
         $P$ need only be a genuine earlier block\\
         of the signature chain\\
         landing status is irrelevant: unlanded,\\
         landed or already final all qualify};
  \node[scsm, scfill, text width=90mm, below=18mm of G] (T2)
        {Tier (ii), recovery (a true stall)\\
         $P$ must satisfy both:\\
         (1) window-final (its candidate has closed)\\
         (2) L1-final (landing tx at $F_{\mathrm{l1}}$ depth)};
  \node[scsm, text width=90mm, below=7mm of T2] (FR)
        {The header carries \texttt{final\_ref} ($\le$ this block's anchor)\\
         proving that $P$ was already $F_{\mathrm{l1}}$-final \emph{at signing time}\\
         (this closes the pre-sign / race-to-land loop at the $F_{\mathrm{l1}}$ boundary)};
  \node[scsm, text width=90mm, below=7mm of FR] (LAND)
        {Also checked at landing: $P$ must be the current canonical landed tip\\
         tip already advanced $\Rightarrow$ benign stranding, rebuild and retry};

  \draw[scarr] (S) -- (G);
  \draw[scarr] (G) -- node[sclab, above=0.4mm] {yes} (T1);
  \draw[scarr] (G) -- node[sclab, left=1mm, align=flush right, text width=30mm]
        {no\\(tier (i) unavailable)} (T2);
  \draw[scarr] (T2) -- (FR);
  \draw[scarr] (FR) -- (LAND);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The decision procedure of the two-tier parent rule. The criterion is the size of the gap on the signature chain, not the landing status of the parent, so the rule evaluates identically at signing time, at proving time and at landing time; only a true stall, whose gap exceeds $G_{\max}$, drops into the recovery tier that demands a final parent.}
\label{fig:4}
\end{figure}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The parent rule --- two tiers, with the criterion being [the size of the gap] rather than the parent's landing status \revnote{26}}: \texttt{parent.slot < s}, and the parent satisfies one of the following. The criterion is the gap \texttt{s − parent.slot}, evaluated over the structure of the signature chain (the gap + \texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash}) --- the evaluation at signing time, at proving time and at landing time agree, and it does not depend on whether the parent "has landed / is already final" at this instant, a state that drifts with time:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{(i) Bounded-gap tier (normal block production)}: \texttt{s − parent.slot ≤ G\_\allowbreak{}max} (the gap cap, initially 64 slot). The parent need only be a real earlier block in the signature chain (\texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash} points at it), and its landing status is immaterial --- not landed, landed but not final, or even already final are all admissible. Normal block production (including the next block after every landing) always takes this tier: after a landing, the next block attaches with a small gap to the (landed) chain head, and a gap ≤ \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} is legal, so "landed but not final" therefore constitutes no coverage hole at all \revnote{27}. This tier guards against deep reorgs: \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} caps the rollback depth of a single block, and a malicious builder that wants to orphan a deeper unlanded tail needs a relayed sparse branch (see the residual risk below).
\item \textbf{(ii) Unbounded-gap tier (recovery)}: \texttt{s − parent.slot} is arbitrary --- but the parent must simultaneously be [window-final] (its candidate has already closed in the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} settlement window)\revnote{28} and [\texttt{L1-\allowbreak{}final}] (\texttt{L1-\allowbreak{}final}, i.e. its landing L1 transaction has reached \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} depth; terminology distinction, review r38 DeepSeek warning 3: "final" here refers specifically to \texttt{L1-\allowbreak{}final} = having reached \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} depth on L1, as distinct from \texttt{landed-\allowbreak{}final} in \hyperref[sec:6.1]{\S6.1} = the window-final finality of an L2 batch that has passed the close of the settlement window \revnote{29} --- the two-tier parent rule always takes \texttt{L1-\allowbreak{}final} as its criterion; for the unification of terminology throughout the document see open item 18(c) of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}). It is entered only when the gap > \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} (tier (i) is unavailable, i.e. a true stall). Why a large gap may be unbounded: by \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2} a final landed block can no longer be reorged, so building on top of it cannot reorg any landed block; it is therefore safe by construction and needs no \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max}. This tier is the only mechanism for recovery (it replaces the entire old re-anchoring episode machinery, see \hyperref[sec:6.4]{\S6.4}). The point of evaluation and the \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} wait \revnote{30}: that the parent "is final and is the current canonical landing tip" is checked by the \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7} landing rule at landing time, not at the moment of signing. Consequently, if a stall begins shortly after a landing and the current landing tip has not yet reached \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}, the recovery block must wait for the current tip to become final (an extra ≤ \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}, on the order of minutes) before it can land --- building on an older final head would fork off the newer tip and be rejected by \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}. A steady-state catastrophic stall (where the last landing became final long ago) incurs no such wait. Determinism likewise falls back on the landing rule: a batch may only extend the current canonical landing tip (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}), and a recovery block built on an old tip, if the tip has since advanced, is benignly stranded and can simply be rebuilt (there is no episode or deposit that could get out of order; during a true stall the tip does not move, so the recovery block is certain to land). \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} makes the predicate "final landed block" stable under shallow L1 reorgs (linked to L1 reorgs in \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}).
\item \textbf{A tier (ii) block header must carry an L1 reference attesting that [at signing time the parent was already \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}-final] --- closing the pre-signing loop at the \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} boundary \revnote{31}}: checking the parent's finality only at landing time hands the aggregator a loop: pre-sign and pre-prove a tier-(ii) block while the tip is not yet final, then race to land it in the first L1 block at which the tip has just become final; this gives one iteration every \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}, the lag never crosses its threshold, and the honest content has not yet aged to \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}prop} and therefore cannot challenge → a sparse forced-only chain lands forever. The fix: the tier (ii) block header gains a new field \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref} = a reference to an L1 block, and the circuit / L1 entry point verifies that "this L1 block has already witnessed the parent reaching \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1} depth", with \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref} ≤ this block's anchor (available at signing time). The parent must therefore already be \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}-final at signing time, and an unfinalized tip cannot be pre-signed; the race-to-land loop is broken (to land a tier (ii) block one must wait until the parent is truly final before signing, and by that time the honest content tail has had time to be proved and to land in the settlement window as a heavier candidate, which by \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} beats the sparse forced-only candidate outright). Normal recovery is unaffected: a recovery block is emitted only after its parent is final anyway.
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Justification for the value \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max\_\allowbreak{}landed = ∞} (direction B)\revnote{32}}: tier (ii) has no cap because the reorg exposure through a landed head is naturally bounded by the length of the unlanded tail, and the tail length is bounded by \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} --- a tail older than \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} has already been landed by the fallback, is final, and can no longer be reorged. "Unbounded" therefore does not increase the worst-case reorg depth (still ≤ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}, the same bound as the existing residual risk in \hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4}), yet it lets a stall of arbitrary duration be recovered in a single hop (see \hyperref[sec:9]{\S9}). A finite value loses at both ends: below the proving latency it can never catch up with a long stall, and set equal to \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} the tightening is an illusion (nature already gives \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}) --- for the full argument see \hyperref[app:D]{Appendix~D}, entry v1.31.
\item \textbf{"tail ≤ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}" is conditional, not unconditional \revnote{33}}: the claim above that "the tail length is bounded by \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}" holds only when permissionless fallback can land an old tail within ≈\texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}, which is exactly the same conditionality as the one \hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3} \revnote{34} attaches to the liveness bound under a Byzantine aggregator. If fallback is economically infeasible (nobody is willing to be a fallback lander) or the fallback landing transaction is itself censored on L1 (already listed in \hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}/\hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2}), the unlanded tail will grow without bound as the stall continues, \texttt{> Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}; in that case \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max\_\allowbreak{}landed = ∞} in tier (ii) lets a single malicious builder orphan this over-long tail with one block (no longer requiring the coalition lookahead density of tier (i), which grows harsher as the tail lengthens), and a colluding lander that lands it thereby orphans honest preconfirmations reaching far beyond \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}. The honest conclusion: the worst-case reorg depth of tier (ii) is ≤ the actual unlanded tail length, and it is ≈ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} if and only if the fallback lands on schedule; when the fallback stalls, the depth grows with the stall, which is the same condition and the same root cause as the possibility in \hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3} that finality stalls indefinitely (no protocol-level DA + fallback economics / L1 censorship resistance). This is not a hole newly introduced by \texttt{∞} (a relayed coalition in tier (i) can equally orphan an entire over-long tail, merely at greater expense), but \texttt{∞} lowers the builder-side cost in that regime to "one person, one block", and this must be spelled out together with the optimistic annotation of \texttt{≤ Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}. r41 note: under the same "someone is willing to land" condition, the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} settlement window turns this kind of orphaning branch into a self-defeating move (a heavier honest tail candidate simply overrides it inside the window), but when "nobody provides the fallback / the fallback is censored on L1" the window may contain only malicious candidates --- the conditionality is unchanged, only the failure mode shifts from "the malicious chain becomes final" to "the malicious chain becomes final in an uncontested window". This is still stated honestly.
\item \textbf{Deep-reorg residual risk \revnote{35}}: no protocol-level DA ⇒ L1 cannot see the unlanded tail ⇒ a colluding lander can always land a legal branch that excludes certain unlanded blocks, orphaning the preconfirmations they contain. The worst case depth = the unlanded tail length ≈ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} + the fallback response time (once a tail is landed it is final). The builder-side cost under the two-tier rule: in tier (i) a single block reaches ≤ \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max − 1}, and going deeper requires a relayed sparse coalition; in tier (ii) a single block already suffices to offer a branch whose parent is a landed head and which orphans at most the entire unlanded tail. But both take effect only when landers collude --- an honest lander follows the \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2} fork choice, lands the heaviest tail and ignores such short branches (see the lander strategy in \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2}); the worst-case depth (≤ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}) and the binding constraint that "lander collusion is required" are both unchanged, and all that changes is that tier (ii) lowers the builder-side cost of a fully orphaning branch from "a dense coalition" to "a single malicious builder". This is a residual risk explicitly accepted under the trust model of \hyperref[sec:1]{\S1} (the cost identity in Appendix A-3), and it breaks only the tier-2 commitment (\hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4}, which already declares that it can be revoked by a single malicious builder). \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} (tier i) remains the governance knob that suppresses the feasibility of a sparse coalition.
\end{itemize}
\subsection[Equivocation slashing (verified directly on L1)]{Equivocation slashing (verified directly on L1)}\label{sec:4.3}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Definition of the fault}: the same builder signs two byte-wise different block headers for the same \texttt{slot}.
\item \textbf{Evidence and enforcement}: anyone may submit the two (block header, signature) pairs to the L1 slashing contract; the contract verifies the two signatures + same slot + different hashes directly on chain --- no zero-knowledge proof and no arbitration period are needed, and once verification passes \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} is slashed, of which ≥ 80\% is burned and the remainder is paid to the submitter (following v15's burn-dominant principle, which prevents self-slashing arbitrage).
\item \textbf{The point at which a slashing takes effect is a deterministic, slot-based rule \revnote{36}}: if the timestamp of the L1 block that includes the slashing transaction corresponds to L2 slot \texttt{s\_\allowbreak{}0}, then the slashing takes effect from slot \texttt{s\_\allowbreak{}0 +\allowbreak{} δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} (initially \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} = 64 slot): once it is in effect, every slot already scheduled to that builder in the lookahead is treated as a gap --- the circuit rule: when a batch lands, the L1 contract hands the list of (slashed party, effective slot) entries it has itself recorded to the proof verification as a verified input; any block within the batch that is "signed by a party whose slashing is already in effect, with slot ≥ the effective slot" is invalid. Effectiveness is delimited by slot (rather than by each block's anchor block), which guarantees that all forks agree on the judgement of "who has already been slashed", so that no anchoring race exists. Without this clause, a slashed builder with a zero bond could still produce legal blocks (\hyperref[sec:4.1]{\S4.1} and \hyperref[sec:5.1]{\S5.1} would then contradict each other).
\item \textbf{Effectiveness must additionally be pinned to [the point of batch acceptance], closing off the backfilling of historical slots \revnote{37}}: the rule above decides purely by \texttt{header.slot ≥ effective slot}, yet \texttt{header.slot} is filled in by the signer itself --- a slashed, zero-bond old key can, after the slashing has taken effect, sign a historical block with \texttt{header.slot < effective slot}, bypass that comparison and keep producing legal blocks (which can also be used to grief with a stale tip, \hyperref[sec:6.4]{\S6.4} / independent review finding 5). The criterion \revnote{38} = the L1 landing time of the [candidate batch] the block belongs to --- inherently unforgeable, replacing the commitment timestamp of v1.40 (the commitment layer was deleted along with the old \hyperref[sec:5.7]{\S5.7}) and the \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}land} guess of v1.39 \revnote{39}: once a slashing has taken effect on L1, no candidate batch whose [L1 landing time ≥ the effective time] may contain a block from that signer --- regardless of what \texttt{header.slot} says; in candidates that had already landed (provisional or window-final) before the effective time, that signer's blocks are grandfathered in as valid. A candidate's landing time is an intrinsic, unforgeable property of the L1 transaction: a historical block backfilled after the fact can only appear in a candidate that lands after the effective time → it is necessarily rejected; a block that had already landed inside some candidate before the effective time → it is retained. The cost (stated honestly)\revnote{40}: that signer's in-flight blocks, signed before the effective time but not yet landed in any candidate by then, are rejected along with the rest --- and the honest successor blocks built on them are stranded in cascade because their \texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash} runs through such a block (when the equivocator broadcasts only one of the two forks, honest successors all build on it; when \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}slash} is shorter than the time to prove the first batch, every candidate that contains it after the effective time is rejected). This still falls within the already-accepted residual risk of "slashable equivocation + ≤ the actual tail depth", but the pricing of \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq} and the user-facing semantics must acknowledge this successor-cascade exposure; no \texttt{δ\_\allowbreak{}land} grace parameter is needed (deleted).
\item This class of fault is submitted by a challenger and verified mechanically by L1: one level stronger than the preconf-vs-record variant in v15 (\hyperref[sec:8]{\S8}b) that relied on an L2 evidence chain --- zero delay after submission, no watchtower needs to run a proof, and all that is required is that someone be willing to submit (there is a bounty, so it is paid permissionless work).
\end{itemize}
\section[The signature chain: validity, canonicality, preconfirmation semantics]{The signature chain: validity, canonicality, preconfirmation semantics}\label{sec:5}
\subsection[Single-block validity (circuit rules)]{Single-block validity (circuit rules)}\label{sec:5.1}
A block is valid if and only if all of the following hold (all of them circuit-verifiable):

\begin{enumerate}
\item The signature is valid and the signer = \texttt{lookahead(\allowbreak{}slot)} (the lookahead is recomputed inside the circuit from L1-anchored data) --- the sole exception: a forced-only block that satisfies the \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7} validity predicate \texttt{P\_\allowbreak{}forced} is exempt from this lookahead check --- \texttt{P\_\allowbreak{}forced} is a precise, circuit-verifiable admission test(any key + the parent is a window-final header [tier (ii) re-rooting] or the preceding forced-only block [tier (i) same-lane continuation)\revnote{41}, replacing the old "three conditions" formulation that was already deleted before v1.31\revnote{42};
\item \texttt{parent.slot < slot}, and the parent block satisfies one of the two tiers of the \hyperref[sec:4.2]{\S4.2} rule\revnote{43}: (i) when the gap satisfies \texttt{slot − parent.slot ≤ G\_\allowbreak{}max}, the parent may be any real earlier block in the signature chain (landing status is irrelevant); (ii) the gap has no upper bound, but the parent must be an L1-final landed block. \texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash} points to that valid parent. A tier (ii) block additionally requires a valid \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref}\revnote{44}: \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref} witnesses that the parent has reached \texttt{F\_\allowbreak{}l1}-final and that \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref} ≤ this block's anchor (\hyperref[sec:4.2]{\S4.2}); that the parent "is the current canonical tip" is still checked by \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7} at landing time (the two predicates are kept separate: at signing time finality is proved by \texttt{final\_\allowbreak{}ref}, at landing time canonicality is verified by the entry point);
\item \texttt{slot} does not run past the range declared by the batch that lands it; the timestamp rule is automatically satisfied (= slot);
\item It includes, under the per-block capacity rule of \hyperref[sec:7]{\S7}, those forced entries that are due and not yet included on chain --- a unified "maximum prefix" definition\revnote{45}: the forced prefix at the head of the block = the longest prefix of the queue's FIFO order that simultaneously satisfies "both the entry count and the gas stay within the \texttt{C\_\allowbreak{}force}/\texttt{C\_\allowbreak{}bridge} share" (\texttt{C\_\allowbreak{}force} is a double cap on entry count and gas, whichever is hit first; the bridge queue works the same way under \texttt{C\_\allowbreak{}bridge}); taking that longest prefix is valid, and only taking less than it is invalid (it is not "exactly some particular count"). The remainder overflows deterministically into subsequent blocks. Consumption is not execution --- an entry that is invalid against the preceding state is consumed and discarded\revnote{46};
\item The execution of the transactions in the block is valid (ordinary state-transition verification); the anchor tx satisfies the freshness rule (\hyperref[sec:8]{\S8}) and satisfies the \hyperref[sec:8]{\S8} causal-ordering invariant \texttt{anchor.L1\_\allowbreak{}timestamp ≤ L2 timestamp(\allowbreak{}slot)}\revnote{47}; and inbound L1→L2 messages are consumed per \hyperref[sec:8]{\S8} up to the [unified maximum prefix] --- the longest prefix of the FIFO order that simultaneously satisfies "count ≤ \texttt{C\_\allowbreak{}anchor}" and "cumulative declared gas ≤ the L1→L2 message gas share"\revnote{48}; taking that longest prefix is valid, and only taking less than it is invalid(this rules out the indefinite censorship of "using a fresh anchor while processing zero inbound messages")\revnote{49}.
\end{enumerate}
\subsection[Canonicality and fork choice]{Canonicality and fork choice}\label{sec:5.2}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The portion already landed on L1\revnote{50}}: the canonical chain = the chain obtained by stringing together the window-final batches at the close of each settlement window (\hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6}). Conflicting candidates within a window are superseded by the \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2} total order, and the close settles the matter for life; only after the close is a candidate "permanently out".
\item \textbf{The coordination convention for the unlanded portion (the P2P tail) --- the ordering criterion is given uniformly by the "best-chain total order" of the next bullet\revnote{51}}: honest builders/nodes build on the chain head that is best under the total order of the next bullet (the old text's "most blocks + highest slot + hash" has been folded into levels 2/3/4 of the total order and is no longer listed separately, so that it cannot conflict with the total order's lane priority). Positioning of its effect\revnote{52}: this is an off-chain P2P coordination convention, not a circuit rule or an L1 entry-point rule --- it constrains only the convergence of honest P2P nodes, and cannot constrain the fork choice of a malicious aggregator / fallback lander / private relay / L1 proposer; the real constraint on malicious landers is given by the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} settlement-window competition (a worse candidate is mechanically overridden by a heavier one), and the bounding of the residual deep-skip exposure is still found in \hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4}.
\item \textbf{The best-chain total order --- shared by three places: fork choice, landing strategy, and the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} window candidate comparison\revnote{53}}. This bullet supersedes the v1.35 "recovery first / frozen dead tail yields" rule --- the independent review (rounds 1/2) pointed out that that rule would order an honest lander to discard a fully visible, landable, healthy long tail, which is wrong: "cannot be extended over P2P" does not mean "should be discarded". Given a fork point \texttt{F}, the candidate chains extending \texttt{F} are compared, in the following order: The object of comparison is a scalar 4-tuple, not a pairwise structural comparison\revnote{54}. Each candidate chain extending \texttt{F} independently computes the 4-tuple \texttt{key = (\allowbreak{}lane,\allowbreak{} count,\allowbreak{} tip\_\allowbreak{}slot,\allowbreak{} tip\_\allowbreak{}hash)}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{\texttt{lane}(candidate-local)\revnote{55}}: = the category of the candidate's first block starting from \texttt{F} --- a discretionary block = the content lane (larger), a forced-only block = the forced-only lane (smaller). Once the first block has fixed it, the whole candidate inherits it and later blocks do not change it\revnote{56};
\item \textbf{\texttt{count}}: the number of valid blocks starting from \texttt{F};
\item \textbf{\texttt{tip\_\allowbreak{}slot}}: the slot of the tip;
\item \textbf{\texttt{tip\_\allowbreak{}hash}}: the block-header hash of the tip(the smaller one wins; this is the last level and settles the tie-break)\revnote{57}. Comparison = the lexicographic order of the 4-tuple (lane descending, count descending, tip\_slot descending, tip\_hash ascending) --- a lexicographic order over scalars is naturally reflexive, total and transitive, so \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6}'s "strictly heavier" relation and the winner at the close are thereby well-defined and independent of submission order. (The formalized property test is listed as item 18 of \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}.) The rationale for content-lane priority is unchanged: forced-only is merely the censorship floor, a content block already bears the forced-prefix obligation, and preferring content sacrifices no censorship resistance. \textbf{A "frozen long tail" is no longer discarded}: a long tail whose head lags the wall clock by more than \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} and can no longer be extended over P2P (no tier-(i) child, and, not being final, no tier-(ii) child either) is still the best chain among the candidates --- the lander should land it first (honoring its preconfirmations) and only then resume producing blocks from its final tip (\hyperref[sec:6.4]{\S6.4}); landing it does not require extending it over P2P, only proving and posting it. The previously feared "deadlock between recovery and fork choice" thereby disappears: first land the best chain (frozen ones included) → then recover in one hop from its final tip.
\end{enumerate}
\item The final arbiter is still L1: disagreements are resolved once and for all by the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} total order at the close of the settlement window\revnote{58}. Equivocators are slashed (\hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3}); blocks that forked innocently because of a network partition become orphans, and the transactions in them return to the mempool to be repacked.
\item \textbf{The lander's tail-selection strategy\revnote{59}}: when a lander faces several candidate tails, it applies the best-chain total order above to its own view and lands the best one. This no longer needs to be a "trusted obligation" --- landing a worse chain gets it directly overridden by a heavier candidate inside the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} window and its cost is spent for nothing, so landing the best chain is the only non-futile strategy; the protocol does not need to judge what a lander "ought to land" (which is exactly what v1.39/v1.40 proved cannot be decided mechanically), it only needs to let the heaviest proven candidate win. \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}prop} is retained as an off-chain strategy reference for the completeness of the lander's view(land the frontier where propagation has fully settled)\revnote{60}, and is no longer any kind of on-chain liability parameter. The handling of "there is a better tail but the lander did not see it"\revnote{61}: some lander did not see the heavier tail and landed a worse candidate? --- that is fine: anyone who does see it lands the heavier tail together with its proof inside the same \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} window and directly overrides it. The incompleteness of a single lander's view is no longer a safety dependency\revnote{62}; overall it is still required that at least one participant inside the window has a complete view and is willing to land --- normal P2P propagation takes seconds and the pending frontier has long since spread everywhere, so this condition is naturally satisfied under the \hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3} fallback feasibility; network-wide eclipse-grade blinding is delegated to the \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12} P2P spec.
\item \textbf{The visibility assumption and the "economic only, not enforced" characterization\revnote{63}}: this design assumes that, under normal P2P propagation, the aggregator (and any lander) can in most cases see the currently longest available signature chain --- but the protocol cannot, and does not attempt to, force a lander to land the longest chain. The reason is structural: visibility of the chain can itself be taken away --- the simplest example is a builder at the tail of the chain withholding the block it has just signed (\hyperref[sec:5.5]{\S5.5} withhold-body / withhold-signature); the aggregator cannot obtain it and therefore cannot include it, and that is not a fault. Hence "land the longest visible chain" is a rational choice under economic incentives, not a slashable obligation: a lander that lands a shorter chain is not penalized, and the consequences run through only two economic channels --- (i) being superseded by a heavier candidate inside the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} window, having paid gas and proving fees for nothing; (ii) under the base-fee revenue-sharing rule below (\hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6}/\hyperref[sec:6.5]{\S6.5}), the shorter the chain landed, the smaller the base on which the share is computed. v1.39/v1.40's attempt to turn "ought to have landed but did not" into an adjudicable fault, and the refutation of that attempt, is precisely the cautionary example for this characterization.
\item \textbf{A node's local chain-selection rule = the total order above(equivocation forks included)\revnote{64}}: the criterion an L2 node uses to dynamically maintain its "local best signature chain" is exactly the 4-tuple total order above; no second set of rules is needed. Equivocation is heavily slashed under \hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3}, but one cannot assume that it does not happen --- when it does, two signed blocks appear at the same slot and the chain splits in two; the two forks are still two individually valid candidate chains, and the 4-tuple yields a total order as usual (differing block counts are settled by \texttt{count}, equal block counts are arbitrated by \texttt{tip\_\allowbreak{}slot}/\texttt{tip\_\allowbreak{}hash}), so the convergence of node views is not interrupted by equivocation; slashing the equivocator (\hyperref[sec:4.3]{\S4.3}) is orthogonal to chain selection. Why the fee total does not enter the total order(a design trade-off)\revnote{65}: it was at one point considered to make "the sum of all base fees on the chain" one of the ordering inputs. The conclusion is that it cannot enter the canonicality criterion: fees are a quantity an attacker can manufacture by paying itself --- a malicious builder that stuffs the block in its own slot with high-fee self-transfers can make a shorter chain beat an honest longer chain on the fee dimension, which amounts to buying out the honest majority's chain-selection outcome with capital (and if part of the fees flows back to the lander, the colluder's cost of manufacturing those fees is even partly refunded). The total order therefore keeps the number of genuinely signed blocks as its primary key (forging one requires stealing a builder's private key and cannot be manufactured); the fee total enters only the reward (the \hyperref[sec:6.5]{\S6.5} base-fee sharing) and not the ordering --- incentive alignment relies on money, canonicality determination relies on signatures.
\item \textbf{Honest bounding\revnote{66}}: before landing, an equivocating builder + one colluder willing to land its fork (the aggregator, or any lander inside the fallback window, or even a bribed L1 proposer) can make the "other" tail canonical and isolate the preconfirmations contained in it. This attack (i) requires one equivocation that L1 can slash directly (the cost = \texttt{L\_\allowbreak{}eq}, priced by the exposure accumulated over the window, \hyperref[sec:4.1]{\S4.1}); (ii) has a depth bound = the length of the unlanded tail. The tail length is not "hard" capped by \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}\revnote{67}: \texttt{lag > Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} only opens the authorization for fallback landing, it does not force any batch to be accepted; while an aggregator that withholds landing drags things out, the tail keeps growing throughout the fallback response time. The honest depth bound = \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} + the fallback response time (one round of proving
\begin{itemize}
\item landing ≈ 10–15 min ≈ 2–3 epochs), and it can be stretched further by sustained L1-level censorship of the fallback transaction --- the latter being the most expensive censorship class, already priced in \hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}. This is a soft upper bound plus an explicitly stated response-time assumption, not a hard cap. Therefore the safety of a preconfirmation before landing is deterrence plus bounded depth, not absolute --- the user-facing semantics of \hyperref[sec:5.4]{\S5.4} are worded accordingly. Keeping the tail short (\hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}) is the principal means of shrinking the payoff of this class of attack.
\end{itemize}
\item \textbf{Canonicality is still determined by L1 alone (G2) --- but L1 does not adjudicate "which chain ought to be landed"; the settlement window selects it mechanically}: off-chain fork choice gives honest nodes convergence (the total order above), while "the best chain wins" is delivered mechanically by the \hyperref[sec:5.6]{\S5.6} settlement window --- L1 does not adjudicate "who ought to land what", it only lets the heaviest proven candidate inside the window become final. This design is therefore not "L1 enforces the longest chain" (that would require protocol-level DA in order to see the unlanded tail) but "L1 picks the heaviest among the already-landed, already-proven candidates" --- no DA, no ex post adjudication; it is the mechanical middle point chosen by the owner under the \hyperref[sec:1]{\S1} constraints\revnote{68}.
\end{itemize}
\subsection[Gaps]{Gaps}\label{sec:5.3}
\begin{itemize}
\item No valid block appears at slot \texttt{s} (the builder went offline, was DoSed, or its block body did not propagate), the chain is left empty at \texttt{s}, and the builder at \texttt{s+\allowbreak{}1} builds on an earlier chain head. Gaps are handled as a normal case, not as an anomaly: no proof of "why it is missing" is required, and nobody is slashed for a gap (producing a block is an opportunity, not an obligation --- inheriting the principle of v15 \hyperref[sec:9.3]{\S9.3}, but now applying to every slot).
\item The cost of a gap is absorbed by incentives: a skipped slot earns no fees, and users who hold preconfirmations settle accounts with that builder's reputation. Nuisance-level chronic absence is handled by the registry's liveness rules (an optional item, \hyperref[sec:12]{\S12}).
\end{itemize}
\subsection[Deletion vs. gaps: what the signature chain fixes and what remains (stated explicitly)]{Deletion vs. gaps: what the signature chain fixes and what remains (stated explicitly)}\label{sec:5.4}
The parent-block-hash chaining fix raised by the owner has precisely the following effect:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Fixed: the aggregator's selective deletion.} Because a child block's signature covers \texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash}, pulling a block out of the middle of the chain breaks the chain for every successor and the proof necessarily fails. Over a signature chain, the aggregator retains only the power to take a prefix: land \texttt{[..m]} and leave \texttt{[m+\allowbreak{}1..k]} outside the chain.(The deep-reorg path in which "a malicious builder starts a short alternative branch from an old point without equivocating, for the aggregator to fork-choose" --- a single block is capped by \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max}, but a relayed sparse branch can reach the entire unlanded tail)\revnote{69}And the suffix that is left behind still attaches to the new L1 chain head, so the next batch (anyone's) can land it just the same --- truncation is delay, not destruction. Together with the landing deadline (\hyperref[sec:6.3]{\S6.3}), a malicious truncation by the aggregator buys little.
\item \textbf{What remains: the skip by an adjacent builder --- and it is profitable\revnote{70}.} Builder \texttt{s+\allowbreak{}1} builds on \texttt{s−1} and claims never to have seen the block at \texttt{s} --- which cannot be falsified inside the protocol ("I did not receive it" is unprovable). v1.4 previously extrapolated "the actor gains nothing" onto skipping, which was wrong: the skipper can repack the high-priority-fee transactions and the MEV from the skipped block \texttt{s} into its own block and still collect the entire revenue of its own slot --- a skip is a profitable, non-slashable revocation of preconfirmations that a single untrusted builder can carry out on its own. Four points of honest bounding: (1) the choice is fixed by a signature and publicly visible (\texttt{parent\_\allowbreak{}hash} in black and white, plus the signed block at \texttt{s} circulating over P2P), so attribution has hard evidence; (2) the one-shot payoff of an adjacent skip is bounded by the fee/MEV transfer of one slot; \textbf{Deep skip / sparse private branch\revnote{71}}: with a single block, one malicious builder can, in a single deep skip, affect at most \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max − 1} unlanded blocks; but a minority coalition that relays several blocks along a private branch(each hop keeping the parent distance ≤ \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max}, every block valid and with no equivocation)\revnote{72}can affect the entire unlanded honest tail. The coordination convention as revised in \hyperref[sec:5.2]{\S5.2} makes the honest network not follow such a fork, so it must be landed on L1 ahead of the others in order to take effect. The crux of the race\revnote{73}: if the colluding lander is only a fallback lander, this really is a race against the honest batch already in flight (the window ≈ the normal landing cadence, and the fallback right does not open at all before \texttt{lag > Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag}); if the colluder is the incumbent aggregator, then during normal operation it is the only routine lander and there is no race to speak of. This combination is a security residual risk that this design prices explicitly, and its depth bound splits into three tiers according to the form of the attack\revnote{74}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Tier (i), single-block deep skip (unlanded parent)}: depth ≤ \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max − 1} (a tail of roughly 1 minute), one malicious slot;
\item \textbf{Tier (i), relayed sparse branch}: depth ≤ the unlanded tail ≈ \texttt{Δ\_\allowbreak{}lag} + the fallback response, at the cost that the coalition must hold one scheduled slot in every \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} window of the tail (the density requirement becomes harsher as \texttt{G\_\allowbreak{}max} is tuned smaller);
\item \textbf{Tier (ii), single block via a landed head\revnote{75}}: one malicious builder produces a block whose parent is a final landed head, and in a single step can supply a branch that isolates up to the entire unlanded tail, with no coalition density required. \textbf{Precondition\revnote{76}}: for that branch to be landable on L1 by a colluding lander, its final parent block must at the same time be the current canonical landed tip (\hyperref[sec:7]{\S7} only lets batches that extend the current tip land) --- that is, the current tip must itself already be final.
